0.975]\\n\\nUnique F1 values per instance: 1-unique=2002 (58%), 2-unique=843, >=3=608\\nExcluded: 2002 constant-F1, 1708 constant-SJ, 1714 constant-EM\\n\\nSelection gain (all 3453 instances):\\n  Random sample F1:  0.6919\\n  SJ-selected F1:    0.7215 (+0.0295)\\n  Oracle best-of-16: 0.7639 (+0.0720)\\n  SJ closes 41.0% of oracle gap\\n\\nAcross-instance rho: SJ=0.483, EM=0.447, FK=0.493\\n\\n\\n## Interpretation for paper/rebuttal\\n\\nThe within-instance utility-F1 Spearman rho is POSITIVE and moderate-to-strong,\\ncontradicting a naive \\\"utility does not correlate with F1 within instances\\\" claim.\\nAmong instances with non-degenerate variation (not all samples identical):\\n- SciERC: median within-rho = 0.47 (SJ), 0.33 (EM) on rich instances\\n- CoNLL: median within-rho = 0.94 (SJ), 0.83 (EM) on rich instances\\n\\nHowever, the more precise framing is:\\n1. A large fraction of instances are DEGENERATE — all N samples produce the same\\n   F1 (SciERC: 21%, CoNLL: 58%). For these, no selection method can help.\\n2. Among instances with within-instance F1 variance, utility IS a positive signal\\n   for identifying better samples.\\n3. The practical selection gain is modest: SJ-based MBR selection closes only\\n   22.6% (SciERC) to 41.0% (CoNLL) of the oracle gap.\\n4. The across-instance rho (0.48-0.55) reflects a DIFFERENT phenomenon: utility\\n   identifies which instances are \\\"easy\\\" (high agreement => high F1), which is\\n   the primary driver of consistency-based selection/filtering methods.\\n\\nSuggested paper framing: \\\"Utility correlates positively with F1 both across and\\nwithin instances, but the practical within-instance selection gain is limited —\\nMBR selection closes only 23-41% of the oracle gap, because (a) most instances\\nshow little within-instance F1 variance, and (b) the correlation, while positive,\\nis imperfect.\\\"\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.35}}"}

FK=0.493\\n\\n\\n## Interpretation for paper/rebuttal\\n\\nThe within-instance utility-F1 Spearman rho is POSITIVE and moderate-to-strong,\\ncontradicting a naive \\\"utility does not correlate with F1 within instances\\\" claim.\\nAmong instances with non-degenerate variation (not all samples identical):\\n- SciERC: median within-rho = 0.47 (SJ), 0.33 (EM) on rich instances\\n- CoNLL: median within-rho = 0.94 (SJ), 0.83 (EM) on rich instances\\n\\nHowever, the more precise framing is:\\n1. A large fraction of instances are DEGENERATE — all N samples produce the same\\n   F1 (SciERC: 21%, CoNLL: 58%). For these, no selection method can help.\\n2. Among instances with within-instance F1 variance, utility IS a positive signal\\n   for identifying better samples.\\n3. The practical selection gain is modest: SJ-based MBR selection closes only\\n   22.6% (SciERC) to 41.0% (CoNLL) of the oracle gap.\\n4. The across-instance rho (0.48-0.55) reflects a DIFFERENT phenomenon: utility\\n   identifies which instances are \\\"easy\\\" (high agreement => high F1), which is\\n   the primary driver of consistency-based selection/filtering methods.\\n\\nSuggested paper framing: \\\"Utility correlates positively with F1 both across and\\nwithin instances, but the practical within-instance selection gain is limited —\\nMBR selection closes only 23-41% of the oracle gap, because (a) most instances\\nshow little within-instance F1 variance, and (b) the correlation, while positive,\\nis imperfect.\\\"\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.35}}"

```text
This is very interesting and important. The results contradict the current paper's framing. Let me analyze carefully:

**Key findings:**

1. **Within-instance utility-F1 ρ is POSITIVE and moderate-to-strong**, NOT near-zero as the paper implies.
   - SciERC: median within-instance SJ-F1 ρ = 0.524 (all non-constant), 0.471 (rich ≥3 unique values)
   - CoNLL shows even stronger correlation with median within-instance SJ-F1 ρ = 0.994 (all non-constant), 0.941 (rich)

2. **The paper currently says** (discussion.tex line 8): "utility correlates with F1 across instances but not within a single instance"
   - This is **WRONG** based on these results. Utility DOES correlate with F1 within instances!

3. **Why selection still fails despite positive within-instance ρ?**
   - Large fraction of instances are DEGENERATE: 21% SciERC, 58% CoNLL have constant F1 across all N samples
   - This degeneracy is what's actually preventing effective selection, not the absence of correlation

4. **The real picture is more nuanced:**
   - Utility does correlate positively within instances, but the practical gains are limited because most instances have little F1 variance to begin with, the correlation isn't perfect, and utility scores often tie
   - SJ-based selection only closes 22.6% to 41.0% of the oracle gap depending on the dataset

5. **There's also a discrepancy I'm noticing** between the selection gains reported in my analysis versus what appears in Table 2 of the paper—the numbers don't align, so I need to investigate where this mismatch is coming from.

The difference likely stems from how the data was filtered and which instances were included. Table 2 appears to use a gold-filtered subset of 529 instances, while my current analysis is working with all 551 instances. There might also be differences in how "random sample" is being calculated versus a single draw, or whether we're comparing against different random seeds.

But here's what's striking: the paper consistently claims that utility doesn't correlate with F1 within instances—it says this in the abstract, introduction, and discussion. Yet when I actually look at the within-instance correlations, they're coming out positive, with median values around 0.47-0.52 for the SJ method. That's a fundamental contradiction between what the paper claims and what the data shows.

I need to dig deeper into why selection still underperforms despite these positive correlations. The data reveals something interesting: about 21% of SciERC instances have constant F1 across all samples, so there's nothing to select from there. Among the remaining instances with actual variation, the median correlation jumps to 0.524. And SJ selection does help—it closes about 22.6% of the oracle gap on SciERC, pushing performance above random. But then Table 2 shows something different, with SJ and Single nearly identical, which suggests there's a discrepancy between what I'm seeing in the raw numbers and what the paper's aggregated results show.

The issue is that "Random" in the MBR analysis means randomly picking one of the 8 samples, which gives an expected F1 of 0.584 on SciERC. But "Single†" in Table 2 is 0.645—much higher. These are measuring different things: one is the mean across all samples, the other is the F1 of a single draw, likely the first or greedy-equivalent sample.

Looking at the actual numbers, Table 2 uses gold-filtered data (n=529) and 2-seed means, which explains why the "Random" baseline there is 0.610 rather than 0.584. But the core question persists: is the within-instance correlation truly near-zero as the paper claims, or is it actually positive? My analysis shows it's positive, which contradicts their claim.

Let me reread what the paper actually means by "utility correlates with F1 across instances but not within"—maybe they're referring to a different metric entirely, not within-instance Spearman ρ.

The paper explicitly states that MBR fails because utility functions correlate with F1 at the instance level but not within single instances. Yet the data clearly shows within-instance correlation is positive. So their causal explanation is incorrect. The real story is more nuanced: within-instance ρ is positive (median around 0.5 on SciERC), but most instances are degenerate with constant or near-constant F1 values, and even with positive correlation, the practical gains remain limited since MBR only closes about 23% of the oracle gap. It's not that utility doesn't correlate within instances—it does—but degeneracy and imperfect correlation severely constrain the practical benefit.

This directly undermines the paper's framing. The MF1 reframe was built on the assumption that utility correlates across instances but not within them, which their own data now contradicts. I need to highlight this discrepancy. Though I should double-check: the paper does report log-probability's within-instance ρ as 0.248 (weak but positive), so they acknowledge some within-instance signal there. But for MBR utility functions like SJ and EM, they claim "not within a single instance"—which is directly contradicted by the new data showing median within-instance SJ-F1 ρ around 0.52. There might be a subtle distinction I'm overlooking here.

Let me clarify what the paper actually means by "MBR utility correlates with F1." Are they claiming that for each instance, the pairwise utility scores correlate with F1 scores within that instance? That's what the analysis found—positive correlation. Or are they making a different claim about whether utility-based selection improves F1? The paper's statement is explicit: "utility correlates with F1 across instances but not within a single instance." That's an empirical claim about correlation, and the data contradicts it.

But there's another layer here. SJ is an instance-level QE score, but for MBR and consensus-proximity selection, the actual utility is LOO-SJ (leave-one-out SJ), which is different. I need to figure out exactly what the analysis is correlating.

Looking at the script name `analyze_within_rho.py` and the summary showing "Within-instance rho (all non-constant instances, n=427/551): SJ: median=0.524," I'm trying to pin down whether they're correlating the per-sample utility scores with per-sample F1 within each instance. If that's the case, then the correlation is positive and the paper's claim is simply wrong. But there's a possibility the paper is conflating instance-level SJ—which is constant across all samples in an instance and can't vary—with per-sample consensus-proximity scores, which do vary.

The paper explicitly states that MBR converts instance-level signals into per-sample rankings, so they acknowledge the per-sample nature of these scores. Yet the data clearly shows a median within-instance correlation of 0.52 between SJ utility and F1 on SciERC, which contradicts their claim that these scores don't correlate with F1 within instances. This is a fundamental issue with their causal narrative that needs addressing. The selection table shows SJ selection at 0.646 versus Single at 0.645—a negligible difference—while the within-instance correlation is supposedly zero, which doesn't add up.

The resolution is that about a fifth of instances have constant F1 across all candidates, making selection impossible on those cases. Among the remaining instances, the correlation is indeed positive, but many have tied utility scores that prevent differentiation. The oracle gap on non-degenerate instances is actually quite small, and when averaged across all instances including the degenerate ones, the net selection gain becomes minimal. The analysis shows MBR closes about 22.6% of the oracle gap overall, so it does provide some benefit on an all-instances basis, but this effect gets diluted by the instances where selection can't help. The discrepancy between the gold-filtered results and the broader analysis suggests different baselines and data filtering are at play.

Now I'm realizing the baseline comparison matters more than I initially thought. The "Random" baseline represents the expected value across random samples, while "Single" is a specific greedy or first draw. If the sampling is truly random with equal probability, these should converge to the same expected F1, but the paper likely uses a fixed seed, introducing variance. The key insight is that the within-instance correlation being positive fundamentally changes how I should interpret this—it's not that utility selection fails conceptually, but rather that practical gains are constrained by the specific instance characteristics and baseline performance. This distinction is crucial for how the paper should frame its findings about the MF1 reframe.

I'm also catching a discrepancy in the reported numbers: the summary claims SciERC's across-instance ρ is 0.549, but the paper's Table 1 shows 0.360 for SJ. The difference appears to stem from the analysis using all 551 instances while the paper reports on gold-filtered data with n=529, and there's also a methodological difference in how SJ and F1 are computed at the instance level.

The key insight though is that within-instance utility-F1 correlation is actually positive (median ρ around 0.47-0.52), which directly contradicts the paper's framing. This matters because the paper's entire causal argument rests on utility failing to correlate with F1, and if that's empirically wrong, the proposed MF1 reframe would perpetuate a flawed narrative. The corrected story is more nuanced but ultimately stronger: utility does correlate with F1 within instances, but selection gains remain limited for other reasons.

mean(F1)\\n    random_f1s = []  # mean F1 per instance\\n    sj_selected_f1s = []  # F1 of the SJ-selected sample\\n    oracle_f1s = []  # F1 of the best sample (oracle)\\n\\n    for inst in instances:\\n        gold = inst[\\\"gold\\\"]\\n        samples = inst[\\\"samples\\\"]\\n        N = len(samples)\\n\\n        f1s = np.array([entity_strict_f1(s.get(\\\"entities\\\", []), gold.get(\\\"entities\\\", [])) for s in samples])\\n\\n        sj_matrix = np.zeros((N, N))\\n        for i, j in combinations(range(N), 2):\\n            v = ner_soft_jaccard_pair(samples[i].get(\\\"entities\\\", []), samples[j].get(\\\"entities\\\", []))\\n            sj_matrix[i, j] = v\\n            sj_matrix[j, i] = v\\n        sj_utilities = sj_matrix.sum(axis=1) / (N - 1) if N > 1 else np.zeros(N)\\n\\n        entity_keys = [entity_set_key(s.get(\\\"entities\\\", [])) for s in samples]\\n        em_utilities = np.zeros(N)\\n        for k in range(N):\\n            em_utilities[k] = sum(1 for j in range(N) if j != k and entity_keys[j] == entity_keys[k]) / (N - 1) if N > 1 else 0.0\\n\\n        fk_val = fleiss_kappa_ner(samples)\\n\\n        instance_mean_f1.append(float(np.mean(f1s)))\\n        instance_mean_sj.append(float(np.mean(sj_utilities)))\\n        instance_mean_em.append(float(np.mean(em_utilities)))\\n        instance_fk.append(fk_val)\\n\\n        n_unique_f1 = len(set(f1s.round(8)))\\n        n_unique_sj = len(set(sj_utilities.round(8)))\\n        n_unique_f1_dist.append(n_unique_f1)\\n        n_unique_sj_dist.append(n_unique_sj)\\n\\n        # Selection gain\\n        best_sj_idx = int(np.argmax(sj_utilities))\\n        sj_selected_f1s.append(float(f1s[best_sj_idx]))\\n        random_f1s.append(float(np.mean(f1s)))\\n        oracle_f1s.append(float(np.max(f1s)))\\n\\n        if np.std(f1s) > 1e-12:\\n            sj_selection_gains.append(float(f1s[best_sj_idx] - <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Good, I've verified the analysis code. The methodology is sound:
- For each instance, it computes LOO-SJ (leave-one-out SJ, which is the MBR utility) and per-sample F1
- It then computes Spearman ρ between SJ utility and F1 within each instance
- Only for instances with non-constant F1 and non-constant SJ (otherwise ρ is undefined)
- "Rich" instances = those with ≥3 unique values on both sides

The results are clear:
- **SciERC**: median within-instance ρ(SJ, F1) = 0.524 (all non-constant), 0.471 (rich)


- **CoNLL**: median within-instance ρ(SJ, F1) = 0.994 (all non-constant), 0.941 (rich)

But there's a major difference in data availability: SciERC loses only 21% of instances to constant F1 values, while CoNLL loses 58%, and the SJ utility shows similar patterns of constant values across both datasets. This means the strong CoNLL correlation is based on a much smaller subset of instances where both metrics actually vary.

The paper's blanket claim that utility correlates with F1 across instances but not within is misleading given these exclusions. Looking at the selection gains, SJ closes 22.6% of the oracle gap on SciERC but 41% on CoNLL—which contradicts the paper's assertion that selection is essentially zero on CoNLL. The discrepancy might be explained by CoNLL being near-ceiling with limited headroom, but I need to dig into the MBR analysis to understand why the selection gains are so different despite the correlation patterns. shows 7.2pp headroom (oracle 0.764 - random 0.692). But the paper's numbers are different because:
1. Paper uses N=8, analysis uses N=16 (more samples → more headroom)
2. Paper uses gold-filtered (n=2756), analysis uses all 3453 instances
3. Paper compares to "Single" (one draw), analysis to "Random" (expected value)

The different baselines and filtering explain the discrepancies. But the core question is: does MBR utility help within instances? Yes, it does — meaningfully so on CoNLL.

However, the paper's comparison is not SJ vs Random — it's SJ vs Single. And in the paper's Table 2 (SciERC), SJ 0.646 vs Single 0.645 = +0.001pp. This is essentially zero.

The gap between "SJ closes 22.6% of gap" and "SJ = +0.001pp" comes down to baseline differences: Random baseline is the mean F1 across all N samples, while Single baseline is F1 from one specific draw. Looking at the paper's code, the "greedy" baseline corresponds to T=0, so the Single baseline (T=1) achieves 0.645 F1 on SciERC.

The analysis uses all 551 instances with a Random baseline of 0.584, while the paper uses gold-filtered 529 instances. This 22-instance difference shifts the baseline and changes how much gap MBR closes. The key insight is that within-instance ρ is positive, MBR does close some oracle gap, but practical gains against the Single baseline are negligible in the paper's results.

Now I see the reconciliation: "Single†" in the paper refers to one T=1.0 draw (not T=0 greedy), so a random T=1 draw averages around 0.584, MBR-selected T=1 achieves 0.620 (a +3.6pp gain), but the specific T=1 sample they report is 0.645—which suggests their particular draw was better than the MBR selection would typically achieve.

The confusion stems from different evaluation sets: the paper reports on 529 gold-filtered SciERC instances while my analysis uses all 551, and I need to clarify whether "random_f1" in the analysis refers to the mean or something else.

But the real issue is the paper's core claim—it states utility correlates with F1 across instances but not within, yet the data shows positive within-instance correlation (median around 0.5 on SciERC). That's a factual discrepancy I need to address in my review.
```

The limitation arises not from lack of signal but from pervasive instance degeneracy: 21-58% of instances produce identical F1 across all N samples, leaving no room for selection, while an additional 13-49% show constant utility scores that prevent ranking.\"\n\n这比\"不相关\"更精确、更可辩护，且更有信息量。\n\n---\n\n### [severity: warning] 分析代码方法学核实\n\n分析脚本 `analyze_within_rho.py` 方法合理：\n- ✅ SJ utility 按标准 LOO-SJ 计算（`sj_matrix.sum(axis=1) / (N-1)`），与 MBR 选择一致\n- ✅ EM utility 按 leave-one-out exact match rate 计算\n- ✅ 正确排除了 constant-F1 和 constant-utility instance（Spearman ρ 未定义）\n- ✅ \"Rich\" 子集要求双边 ≥3 unique values，更保守\n\n但有一个方法学注意：\n- SciERC 使用 N=8（exp_012_rerun_1024），CoNLL 使用 N=16（exp_002_conll_n16_r1024）\n- N=16 比 N=8 产生更多 unique values → CoNLL 的高 ρ 部分源于更多 sample 提供更好的 rank estimation\n- 论文如要引用这些数字，需注明 N 的差异\n\n---\n\n### [severity: info] 选择增益数据有参考价值但与 paper Table 2 基线不同\n\n分析报告 \"SJ closes 22.6% of oracle gap\"（SciERC：SJ-selected 0.620 vs Random 0.584）。但 Table 2 显示 SJ=0.646 vs Single=0.645（差 0.001pp）。差异原因：\n1. 分析用全部 551 instance，Table 2 用 gold-filtered 529\n2. \"Random\" = mean(sample F1)，\"Single\" = 一次特定 T=1 draw 的 F1\n3. 2-seed mean vs 单 seed\n\n不建议直接用分析中的选择增益数字替代 Table 2，但 \"23-41% oracle gap closed\" 可以作为 Discussion 中的定量描述。\n\n---\n\n### 建议\n\n1. **立即修正三处 \"not within\" claim**：改为 \"utility correlates positively within instances but selection gains are limited by pervasive instance degeneracy\"\n2. **MF1 reframe 围绕 degeneracy 机制重构**：Contribution 1 = \"MBR closes only 23-41% of oracle gap despite positive within-instance correlation, because 21-58% of instances are degenerate\"\n3. **添加 degeneracy 统计到 paper**：在 Discussion 或 Appendix 加一个小表：instances with constant F1 (%) + constant utility (%) per dataset\n4. **重新审视 MF1 reframe 方案后再 dispatch Paper Agent**——不要在错误前提下写作",

- Greedy T=0 (0.663) > LP selection (0.647) by 1.6pp ✓

- Greedy T=0: 0.4289

- Paper Table 2: LP 0.395, Single† 0.404

**CoNLL NER (N=8, Qwen2.5-7B):**
- Greedy T=0: 0.9074
- Mean sample F1: 0.8665
- The greedy approach also outperforms the paper's single-sample baseline here.

Looking at the paper's footnote, "Single†" refers to one T=1.0 draw, not greedy T=0 selection. So the comparison is between my greedy T=0 results and their stochastic sampling approach, which explains the consistent improvements across tasks. of all T=1 draws: 0.608 (from "Sample F1 mean")

- Best selection method (LP): 0.647
- Gap: T=0 greedy wins by 1.6pp

- Qwen CoNLL NER: Greedy 0.907 > mean sample 0.867
- ALL: Greedy always dominates mean sample F1 by a significant margin

The data checks out. Now I need to verify against Table 2 in the paper and reconcile a naming discrepancy — the extraction shows "Qwen2.5-7B" but the paper references "Qwen3-8B", which might just be a difference between the HuggingFace model identifier and the paper's naming convention.
